# Chemistry — Questions

Answers: [answers.md](answers.md). Question numbers match answer numbers.

## VCE Units 1 & 2

### Unit 1 AOS 1: Elements, bonding and properties

#### Atomic structure and isotopes

**Q1.** How many neutrons are in one atom of chlorine-37 (³⁷₁₇Cl)?
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-1 -->

- A. 17
- B. 20
- C. 37
- D. 54

**Q2.** Carbon-12 and carbon-14 have the same chemical properties because they have the same
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-2 -->

- A. mass number.
- B. number of neutrons.
- C. number of nucleons.
- D. number and arrangement of electrons.

**Q3.** The ion ⁵⁶Fe³⁺ contains
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-3 -->

- A. 26 protons, 30 neutrons and 23 electrons.
- B. 26 protons, 30 neutrons and 29 electrons.
- C. 23 protons, 33 neutrons and 26 electrons.
- D. 26 protons, 56 neutrons and 23 electrons.

**Q4.** A sample of copper contains 69.2% copper-63 and 30.8% copper-65 (take the isotopic masses as 63.0 and 65.0). The relative atomic mass of copper in this sample is closest to
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-4 -->

- A. 63.0
- B. 63.6
- C. 64.0
- D. 64.4

**Q5.** In Rutherford's gold foil experiment, most alpha particles passed straight through the foil, but a very small number were deflected back. This showed that
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-5 -->

- A. electrons occupy fixed energy levels.
- B. atoms are solid, indivisible spheres.
- C. an atom is mostly empty space with a small, dense, positively charged nucleus.
- D. negative charges are spread evenly through a positive sphere.

**Q6.** Define the term isotopes and explain why ¹²C and ¹⁴C react identically with oxygen. (2 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-6 -->

*Written answer*

**Q7.** Naturally occurring boron is 19.9% ¹⁰B (isotopic mass 10.0) and 80.1% ¹¹B (isotopic mass 11.0). Calculate the relative atomic mass of boron to three significant figures. Show your working. (2 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-7 -->

*Written answer*

**Q8.** A particle contains 16 protons, 18 neutrons and 18 electrons. Identify the particle and write its full symbol, including mass number and charge. Explain how you determined the charge. (3 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-8 -->

*Written answer*

#### Electron configuration and subshells

**Q9.** Which is the correct ground-state electron configuration of a potassium atom?
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-1 -->

- A. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 3d¹
- B. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁷
- C. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁵ 4s²
- D. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 4s¹

**Q10.** The maximum number of electrons that can occupy a d subshell is
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-2 -->

- A. 2
- B. 6
- C. 10
- D. 14

**Q11.** The ground-state electron configuration of copper is an exception to the usual filling order. It is
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-3 -->

- A. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 3d¹⁰ 4s¹
- B. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 3d⁹ 4s²
- C. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 3d⁸ 4s² 4p¹
- D. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 4s² 4p⁹

**Q12.** The electron configuration of the Fe³⁺ ion is
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-4 -->

- A. [Ar] 3d³ 4s²
- B. [Ar] 3d⁵
- C. [Ar] 3d⁶ 4s²
- D. [Ar] 3d⁴ 4s¹

**Q13.** The outermost subshell of an element's ground-state atom is 4p³. The element is in
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-5 -->

- A. period 3, group 15 of the p-block.
- B. period 4, group 13 of the p-block.
- C. period 4, group 15 of the p-block.
- D. period 4, group 5 of the d-block.

**Q14.** Write the full subshell electron configuration for a sulfur atom and for the sulfide ion, S²⁻. (2 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-6 -->

*Written answer*

*Written answer*

**Q16.** When a sample of a lithium salt is placed in a flame, a crimson colour is seen. Through a spectroscope this light appears as a series of distinct coloured lines. Explain how these lines arise and why each element gives a unique pattern. (3 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-8 -->

#### Periodic table trends

**Q17.** Moving from sodium to chlorine across period 3, atomic radius decreases. The best explanation is that
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-1 -->

- A. the number of occupied shells decreases.
- B. core charge increases while the number of occupied shells stays the same.
- C. the number of neutrons increases, pulling electrons closer.
- D. electronegativity decreases across the period.

**Q18.** Which period 3 element has the highest first ionisation energy?
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-2 -->

- A. Sodium
- B. Magnesium
- C. Chlorine
- D. Argon

**Q19.** The core charge experienced by the valence electrons of a sulfur atom is
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-3 -->

- A. +2
- B. +6
- C. +10
- D. +16

**Q20.** Potassium reacts more vigorously with water than sodium does. This is mainly because, in potassium,
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-4 -->

- A. the valence electron is further from the nucleus, so it is lost more easily.
- B. the core charge is greater.
- C. there are more valence electrons.
- D. the atom has a smaller atomic radius.

**Q21.** Element X has a core charge of +7 and three occupied electron shells. Compared with fluorine, X would be expected to
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-5 -->

- A. have a higher electronegativity.
- B. have a smaller atomic radius.
- C. be less reactive and have a lower electronegativity.
- D. lose electrons more readily to form positive ions.

**Q22.** The ions Na⁺ and F⁻ both have the electron configuration 1s² 2s² 2p⁶. Explain why Na⁺ has a smaller radius than F⁻. (2 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-6 -->

*Written answer*

**Q23.** Describe and explain the trend in first ionisation energy down group 1 from lithium to caesium. (3 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-7 -->

*Written answer*

**Q24.** The electronegativities of four period 3 elements are: Na 0.9, Mg 1.3, P 2.2, Cl 3.2. Describe the trend shown and explain it in terms of atomic structure. (2 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-8 -->

*Written answer*

#### Metallic bonding and properties of metals

**Q25.** In the metallic bonding model, a metal consists of
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-1 -->

- A. a lattice of positive ions surrounded by a sea of delocalised electrons.
- B. a lattice of alternating positive and negative ions.
- C. discrete molecules held together by dispersion forces.
- D. atoms that share localised electron pairs with neighbours.

**Q26.** Metals are malleable because
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-2 -->

- A. the ions are held in fixed positions by directional bonds.
- B. the delocalised electrons evaporate when the metal is struck.
- C. layers of cations can slide over each other without breaking the non-directional metallic bonding.
- D. metals contain weak intermolecular forces between layers.

**Q27.** Which property of metals is NOT well explained by the simple metallic bonding model?
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-3 -->

- A. Electrical conductivity
- B. Ductility
- C. Thermal conductivity
- D. The magnetic behaviour of iron, cobalt and nickel

**Q28.** A piece of steel is heated until red hot and then allowed to cool slowly in air. This treatment, called annealing, produces steel that is
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-4 -->

- A. harder and more brittle, with very small crystals.
- B. softer and more ductile, with larger crystals.
- C. harder because carbon atoms leave the lattice.
- D. unchanged in properties.

**Q29.** Metals conduct electricity in the solid state because
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-5 -->

- A. the cations move towards the negative electrode.
- B. the delocalised electrons can move through the lattice when a potential difference is applied.
- C. metal atoms ionise only when a voltage is applied.
- D. the metal contains free negative ions.

**Q30.** Magnesium melts at 650 °C, whereas sodium melts at 98 °C. Use the metallic bonding model to explain this difference. (2 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-6 -->

*Written answer*

**Q31.** Steel is an interstitial alloy of iron containing small amounts of carbon. Explain why steel is harder than pure iron. (2 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-7 -->

*Written answer*

**Q32.** A blacksmith quenches a hot steel blade in cold water and then tempers it. Describe how each treatment changes the crystal structure and properties of the steel. (3 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-8 -->

*Written answer*

#### Ionic bonding and ionic compounds

**Q33.** The correct formula for aluminium sulfate is
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-1 -->

- A. AlSO₄
- B. Al₃(SO₄)₂
- C. Al₂(SO₄)₃
- D. Al₂SO₄

**Q34.** Ionic crystals shatter when struck with a hammer because
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-2 -->

- A. the delocalised electrons are knocked out of the lattice.
- B. the blow shifts layers so that ions of like charge align and repel.
- C. the covalent bonds within the ions break.
- D. the intermolecular forces between ions are weak.

**Q35.** Which of the following compounds would be expected to have the highest melting point?
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-3 -->

- A. Sodium chloride, NaCl
- B. Potassium bromide, KBr
- C. Caesium iodide, CsI
- D. Magnesium oxide, MgO

**Q36.** When magnesium reacts with oxygen to form magnesium oxide, each magnesium atom
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-4 -->

- A. shares two electrons with an oxygen atom.
- B. transfers two electrons to an oxygen atom.
- C. gains two electrons from an oxygen atom.
- D. transfers one electron to each of two oxygen atoms.

**Q37.** Solid potassium chloride does not conduct electricity, but molten potassium chloride does. This is because, in the molten state,
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-5 -->

- A. electrons become delocalised.
- B. the ions are free to move and carry charge.
- C. covalent bonds break to form atoms.
- D. K⁺ ions gain electrons to become atoms.

**Q38.** Calcium reacts with nitrogen to form calcium nitride. Write the formula of calcium nitride and explain its formation in terms of electron transfer. (3 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-6 -->

*Written answer*

<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-7 -->

*Written answer*

**Q40.** A student finds that an unknown white solid has a high melting point, does not conduct electricity as a solid but conducts when dissolved in water, and shatters when struck. Identify the type of bonding present and explain how any two of these observations support your answer. (3 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-8 -->

<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-2 -->

### Unit 1 AOS 1: Covalent substances

#### Lewis structures and covalent bonding

**Q49.** How many non-bonding (lone) pairs of electrons are on the nitrogen atom in an ammonia molecule, NH₃?
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-1 -->

- A. 0
- B. 1
- C. 2
- D. 3

- A. O₂
- B. H₂O
- C. N₂
- D. CH₄

<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-3 -->

- A. 2
- B. 4
- C. 6
- D. 8

<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-4 -->

- A. 4
- B. 5
- C. 6
- D. 8

<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-5 -->

- A. the electrostatic attraction between oppositely charged ions.
- B. the attraction between a lattice of cations and delocalised electrons.
- C. the attraction between neighbouring molecules.
- D. the electrostatic attraction between a shared pair of electrons and the nuclei of the two bonded atoms.

#### Intermolecular forces

**Q73.** The strongest type of intermolecular force between methanol molecules, CH₃OH, is
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-1 -->

- A. dispersion forces.
- B. hydrogen bonding.
- C. dipole–dipole attraction without hydrogen bonding.
- D. covalent bonding.

**Q74.** The boiling points of the noble gases increase from helium to xenon. The best explanation is that
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-2 -->

- A. the atoms become more polar down the group.
- B. hydrogen bonding increases down the group.
- C. the larger number of electrons gives stronger dispersion forces.
- D. covalent bonds form between larger atoms.

**Q75.** Which substance CANNOT form hydrogen bonds between its own molecules?
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-3 -->

- A. Hydrogen fluoride, HF
- B. Ammonia, NH₃
- C. Methanol, CH₃OH
- D. Chloromethane, CH₃Cl

<!-- id: chem12a1-im-4 -->

- A. pentane forming hydrogen bonds.
- B. the compact shape of 2,2-dimethylpropane reducing contact between molecules, so it has weaker dispersion forces.
- C. 2,2-dimethylpropane having fewer electrons.
- D. pentane being a polar molecule.

<!-- id: chem12a1-im-5 -->

- C. H₂S has fewer electrons than H₂O.

<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-1 -->

- B. covalently bonded to four other carbon atoms in a tetrahedral arrangement.

<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-2 -->

- A. each carbon atom has one delocalised electron that can move along the layers.

<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-4 -->

<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-5 -->

### Unit 1 AOS 2: Quantifying and classifying materials

#### Relative masses and mass spectrometry

**Q89.** Chlorine has two naturally occurring isotopes, ³⁵Cl and ³⁷Cl, and a relative atomic mass of 35.45. Taking the relative isotopic masses as 35 and 37, what is the percentage abundance of ³⁵Cl?
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-1 -->

- A. 22.5%
- B. 50.0%
- C. 75.5%
- D. 77.5%

**Q90.** In a mass spectrometer, what is actually detected and used to produce the mass spectrum?
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-2 -->

- A. Neutral atoms, separated according to their mass
- B. Positively charged ions, separated according to their mass-to-charge ratio
- C. Electrons removed from the sample, separated according to their energy
- D. Negatively charged ions, separated according to their size

**Q91.** Relative isotopic mass and relative atomic mass are both measured on a scale relative to:
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-3 -->

- A. one-twelfth of the mass of an atom of carbon-12
- B. the mass of one hydrogen-1 atom
- C. the mass of one proton
- D. one-sixteenth of the mass of an atom of oxygen-16

**Q92.** Gallium consists of ⁶⁹Ga (60.1%) and ⁷¹Ga (39.9%). Taking the relative isotopic masses as 69 and 71, the relative atomic mass of gallium is closest to:
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-4 -->

- A. 69.4
- B. 69.8
- C. 70.0
- D. 70.2

**Q93.** Bromine exists as ⁷⁹Br and ⁸¹Br in almost equal amounts. The mass spectrum of Br₂ shows molecular-ion peaks at m/z 158, 160 and 162 in a ratio of about 1 : 2 : 1. The best explanation for the tallest peak at 160 is that:
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-5 -->

- A. 160 is the relative molecular mass of the most abundant isotope of bromine
- B. the ⁸¹Br–⁸¹Br molecule is the most stable
- C. a ⁷⁹Br–⁸¹Br molecule can form in two ways (⁷⁹Br first or ⁸¹Br first), so it is twice as likely as either ⁷⁹Br–⁷⁹Br or ⁸¹Br–⁸¹Br
- D. Br₂⁺ ions with m/z 160 are deflected the least

**Q94.** Magnesium has three naturally occurring isotopes: ²⁴Mg (78.99%), ²⁵Mg (10.00%) and ²⁶Mg (11.01%). Taking the relative isotopic masses as 24, 25 and 26, calculate the relative atomic mass of magnesium to four significant figures. (2 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-6 -->

*Written answer*

**Q95.** Distinguish between relative isotopic mass and relative atomic mass, and explain why the relative atomic mass of most elements is not a whole number. (3 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-7 -->

*Written answer*

**Q96.** The mass spectrum of a metallic element X shows only two peaks: m/z 63 with a relative abundance of 69.2% and m/z 65 with a relative abundance of 30.8%. Calculate the relative atomic mass of X and identify the element. (3 marks)
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-8 -->

*Written answer*

#### The mole and molar mass

<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-1 -->

- A. 6.02 × 10²³
- B. 1.51 × 10²³
- C. 2.41 × 10²⁴
- D. 4.15 × 10⁻²⁵

<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-2 -->

- A. 102.1 g mol⁻¹
- B. 164.1 g mol⁻¹
- C. 150.1 g mol⁻¹
- D. 204.1 g mol⁻¹

#### Stoichiometry and limiting reactants

- A. H₂ is the limiting reactant
- B. O₂ is the limiting reactant
- C. Neither is in excess; they are in stoichiometric amounts

<!-- id: chem-1-4-3 -->

#### Collision theory and reaction rates

#### Le Chatelier's principle and yield optimisation

**Q520.** For N₂(g) + 3H₂(g) ⇌ 2NH₃(g), ΔH = −92 kJ mol⁻¹, the volume of the vessel is decreased at constant temperature. How does the system respond?
<!-- id: chem-3-4-1 -->

- A. The equilibrium shifts to the left, because there are more gas particles on the reactant side
- B. There is no shift, because pressure does not affect gaseous equilibria
- C. The equilibrium shifts to the right, because the reaction is exothermic
- D. The equilibrium shifts to the right, because there are fewer gas particles on the product side

**Q521.** The temperature of an equilibrium mixture of N₂, H₂ and NH₃ (ΔH = −92 kJ mol⁻¹) is increased. Which statement is correct?
<!-- id: chem-3-4-2 -->

- A. The rate and the equilibrium yield of ammonia both increase
- B. The rate decreases but the equilibrium yield of ammonia increases
- C. The rate increases but the equilibrium yield of ammonia decreases
- D. The rate increases and the yield of ammonia is unchanged

**Q522.** The Haber process is typically run at about 400–450 °C, even though lower temperatures give a higher equilibrium yield. This is because:
<!-- id: chem-3-4-3 -->

- A. the reaction is endothermic, so high temperatures increase yield

<!-- id: chem34a2-phys-1 -->

- A. Butane, C₄H₁₀
- B. Methyl methanoate, HCOOCH₃
- C. Propan-1-ol, C₃H₇OH
- D. Ethanoic acid, CH₃COOH

**Q818.** Why do boiling points increase from methane to octane in the alkane homologous series?
<!-- id: chem34a2-phys-2 -->

- A. Hydrogen bonding increases with chain length.
- B. Longer molecules have more electrons and greater surface area, so dispersion forces are stronger.
- C. The C–C covalent bonds become stronger.
- D. Longer alkanes are more polar.

**Q819.** Pentane boils at 36 °C and its isomer 2,2-dimethylpropane boils at 10 °C. What is the best explanation?
<!-- id: chem34a2-phys-3 -->

- A. 2,2-dimethylpropane has a lower molar mass.
- B. Pentane can form hydrogen bonds.
- C. The compact, branched shape of 2,2-dimethylpropane gives less surface contact, so its dispersion forces are weaker.
- D. Branching makes the C–H bonds weaker.

**Q820.** Which statement explains why the solubility of alcohols in water decreases from methanol to hexan-1-ol?
<!-- id: chem34a2-phys-4 -->

- A. The longer non-polar hydrocarbon chain disrupts hydrogen bonding with water and cannot itself interact strongly with water.
- B. The –OH group becomes less polar as the chain gets longer.
- C. Larger alcohols react with water.
- D. Larger alcohols have fewer hydrogen atoms.

**Q821.** Moving from hexane to decane in the alkane series, how do viscosity and flash point change?
<!-- id: chem34a2-phys-5 -->

- A. Viscosity decreases; flash point decreases.
- B. Viscosity increases; flash point decreases.
- C. Viscosity decreases; flash point increases.
- D. Viscosity increases; flash point increases.

**Q822.** Ethanol (M = 46 g mol⁻¹) boils at 78 °C, while propane (M = 44 g mol⁻¹) boils at −42 °C. Explain this difference with reference to intermolecular forces. (3 marks)
<!-- id: chem34a2-phys-6 -->

*Written answer*

<!-- id: chem34a2-phys-7 -->

*Written answer*

#### Mass spectrometry

#### Infrared spectroscopy

- B. a C–H bond in an alkane

### Green chemistry & sustainability

#### Green chemistry principles

**Q1097.** Which statement best describes the green chemistry principle of 'prevention'?
<!-- id: chem-8-1-1 -->

- A. It is better to prevent waste from forming than to treat or clean it up after it has formed.
- B. Hazardous waste should be stored securely on site until it can be incinerated.
- C. All waste products should be diluted before release to the environment.
- D. Pollution controls should be added at the end of a production line.
